--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'll analyze the aggregation mission carefully.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:** Score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region (BLACK floor strip bordered by BLUE walls). Robots start scattered in the center (GRAY floor). I need robots to move to the BLACK strip with BLUE walls and stay there.

**Key perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK = target strip OR wrong strip, GRAY = middle area
- Wall color: BLUE = target side, GREEN = wrong side
- The robot can see colored walls with camera (C=7)

**Strategy:**
- When on GRAY floor: move toward BLUE walls (GoToColor with blue=3... wait, colors 1-6. Blue walls = color? Let me check: clr options are {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Blue is likely color 3 or similar)
- When on BLACK floor: check if near BLUE walls (good) or GREEN walls (bad)
- If on BLACK + see BLUE walls: stop/stay
- If on BLACK + see GREEN walls: go toward BLUE

The wall colors: BLUE walls border the target strip. Color IDs: typically 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So BLUE=3, GREEN=2.

**FSM design:**
- State 0: Explore/GoToColor(blue) - move toward blue walls
- State 1: Stop - we're in target region (BLACK floor + see BLUE)
- Transition from 0→1: floor is BLACK AND see blue (use BLACK floor condition)
- Transition from 1→0: floor is GRAY or see GREEN

Let me design 14 machines with varying strategies:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know what worked. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify my approach.

## Reasoning

**Mission recap:** Robots need to reach the BLACK floor strip bordered by BLUE walls. GRAY = middle (bad), BLACK = strip (could be target or wrong side), BLUE walls = target side, GREEN walls = wrong side.

**Key insight I may have gotten wrong:** Color IDs in AutoMoDe - let me reconsider. The arena has BLUE walls (target) and GREEN walls (other side). Camera perceives colored objects. Blue=3? Green=2? These are the most natural mappings.

**Better strategy:** 
- Use GoToColor(blue=3) to navigate toward blue walls
- Use GoAwayColor(green=2) to flee green walls  
- On BLACK floor + see BLUE → Stop
- On BLACK floor + see GREEN → flee green / go toward blue

**State designs:**
- 2-state: GoToBlue → Stop when BLACK floor
- 3-state: Explore → GoToBlue (when see blue) → Stop (when BLACK)
- Include GoAwayColor(green) as alternative navigation

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received any score feedback. I need to reason more carefully about what might be working and diversify more aggressively.

## Reasoning

**Re-examining the problem:** 
- BLACK floor = both strips (target AND wrong strip)
- GRAY floor = middle (definitely bad)
- BLUE walls = border the TARGET strip
- GREEN walls = border the WRONG strip

**Critical insight:** When a robot is on BLACK floor, it could be in EITHER strip. It needs to distinguish using wall color perception (C=7). The condition C=0 (BLACK floor) fires in BOTH strips, so stopping on BLACK is wrong ~50% of the time.

**Better logic:**
- If seeing BLUE walls → move toward them (GoToColor blue=3) or stay if already there
- If seeing GREEN walls → flee them (GoAwayColor green=2)  
- If on GRAY → move toward BLUE walls
- If on BLACK + see BLUE → STOP (we're in the right place)
- If on BLACK + see GREEN → flee GREEN

**Important:** Can a robot on the correct BLACK strip still see GREEN walls? Possibly not if far enough. Need to use both floor AND wall color conditions together.

**Also reconsidering color IDs:** In ARGoS, LED/wall colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So green walls=2, blue walls=3. That seems right.

**New approach - use C=7 (see blue) as primary signal for stopping:**

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 3 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 0 --p0x1 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 1 --p1x0 0.9 --n1x1 0 --c1x1 7 --l1x1 2 --p1x1 0.9

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit score feedback. Let me reconsider the problem more carefully and try fundamentally different approaches.

## Reasoning

**Re-reading the mission:** This says "AGGREGATION mission" but the scoring description says "score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region." That's actually a FORAGING/GATHERING to region task, not classic aggregation (which is about clustering robots together).

**Critical re-examination of color IDs:** In AutoMoDe/ARGoS, the color IDs for the camera condition C=7 correspond to LED colors robots emit. But WALLS have colors too. The wall colors BLUE and GREEN - are these perceivable? The condition C=7 says "perceives a given colour with its camera." Wall colors should be visible.

**What colors are the walls?** BLUE walls border target, GREEN walls border wrong strip. In ARGoS color encoding: I've been assuming blue=3, green=2. Let me also try other mappings.

**New strategy - be more aggressive about staying in target:**
- The floor is BLACK in both strips. The key differentiator is wall color.
- A robot in the GRAY area should move toward BLUE walls.
- A robot seeing GREEN walls should flee.
- The simplest robust FSM: GoAwayColor(GREEN) always - this pushes robots toward the BLUE side.

**Key insight I've been missing:** GoAwayColor(green=2) from ANYWHERE will push robots toward the blue wall side! This might be the simplest effective strategy.

Also trying: what if the wall color IDs are different? Let me try clr=1 (red?), clr=4 (yellow?), clr=5 (magenta?), clr=6 (cyan?) for blue walls in case blue=6 (cyan) or similar.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm not receiving score feedback which makes optimization very difficult. Let me fundamentally reconsider the problem.

## Reasoning

**Re-reading carefully:** "AGGREGATION mission" - but the score description says robots outside the TARGET REGION score points. This is actually a region-finding task. The target is the BLACK strip with BLUE walls.

**What I might be getting wrong:**
1. Color IDs: Maybe BLUE walls = color 6 (cyan) or color 3. GREEN walls = color 2 or color 4.
2. Maybe the GoToColor/GoAwayColor behaviors target robot LEDs, not walls? If so, wall colors aren't detectable this way.
3. Maybe C=7 (perceives color with camera) CAN see walls.

**Fundamental rethink:** If wall colors aren't reliably detectable, the only floor-based signal is: BLACK = in a strip, GRAY = in the middle. But BOTH strips are black!

**Alternative approach:** What if we use the fact that the target strip is at one END of the arena? Robots could use boundary-following or spiral to reach walls, then follow along. But there's no "wall detection" per se.

**Simplest possible approaches to try:**
- Pure GoToColor(blue) - just always move toward blue = 3 or 6
- Pure GoAwayColor(green) - just always flee green = 2 or 4  
- On BLACK floor → stop (accepts ~50% wrong, but at least stays in strips)
- Random walk with very slow probability of stopping on BLACK

**Let me try ALL color IDs for both GoToColor and GoAwayColor to cover all mappings:**

Also: maybe the scoring is actually aggregation (robots near each other) and I'm misreading. "Score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region" - no, it's clear.

**Key new idea:** What if I use a 1-state machine that just always does GoToColor targeting every possible color? And also try pure Exploration with stop on BLACK.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been running 6 rounds without any score feedback. Let me completely rethink from scratch with much more systematic exploration.

## Reasoning

**The core problem I haven't solved:** Without score feedback, I don't know which designs work. I need to be much more systematic.

**Fundamental logic re-examination:**
- BLACK floor = BOTH strips (target AND wrong). So "stop on BLACK" sends half robots to wrong strip.
- GRAY floor = middle area (all robots start here, all bad).
- The ONLY way to distinguish strips is via wall color: BLUE = target side, GREEN = wrong side.
- C=7 (camera perceives color) should detect wall colors since walls are colored objects.

**What if C=7 doesn't work for walls?** Then robots have NO way to distinguish strips, and the best strategy might be: reach ANY black strip quickly and stay. This gives ~50% of robots in target = score ~600. But can we do better?

**What if the robots' own LEDs matter?** If robots emit colors (via --cle), other robots can see them. This enables coordination - robots in target region emit a color, others navigate toward it.

**New strategy: LED-based beacon approach:**
- Robots in BLACK strip emit BLUE (cle=3... wait cle options are {0,4,5,6}: 0=off,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan)
- Other robots move toward that LED color
- This could allow "follow the crowd" to the target

**Let me try LED beacon strategies AND pure movement strategies:**

State 0: Explore (GRAY floor) → when see BLACK floor, go to State 1
State 1: Stop + emit yellow LED → other robots see yellow and approach

Also trying: FlockCenter (B=10) to aggregate robots, combined with GoToColor.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been running 7 rounds with no score feedback at all. Let me take a completely different systematic approach.

## Reasoning

**What I know for certain:**
- Robots start on GRAY (middle). Score = robots outside target BLACK strip.
- BLACK floor exists in BOTH strips - can't use floor color alone to identify target.
- BLUE walls = target side, GREEN walls = wrong side.
- C=7 detects colors seen by camera - this SHOULD detect wall colors.

**What might be failing:**
1. Maybe wall colors don't correspond to standard color IDs 1-6 as I assumed
2. Maybe the floor-BLACK condition (C=0) is unreliable for distinguishing strips
3. Maybe my LED beacon idea is promising but needs refinement

**Completely new systematic approach - try EVERY combination of:**
- GoToColor with each color 1-6
- GoAwayColor with each color 1-6  
- Simple stop-on-black
- LED beacons

**Most important new idea:** The problem says "AGGREGATION mission" - maybe I've been wrong about what's being scored! Maybe it IS classic aggregation (robots clustering together) and the "target region" description was just how they phrased the scoring. If so, Attraction (B=4) and FlockCenter (B=10) behaviors are key!

Let me try pure aggregation behaviors combined with floor-based stopping, AND wall-color navigation:

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've run 8 rounds with zero score feedback. I need to completely rethink my approach.

## Reasoning

**Critical realization:** I've been assuming the scoring description is about finding a colored strip. But re-reading: "AGGREGATION mission" - the standard AutoMoDe aggregation task has robots aggregate together (cluster). The "target region" may just be wherever the swarm clusters. But the problem says "strip with BLUE walls" explicitly.

**What I think is happening:** My designs may all be performing similarly poorly because:
1. The C=7 condition may not detect wall colors (walls may not be "colored objects" in the camera sense)
2. The floor condition C=0 (BLACK) triggers in BOTH strips, causing ~50% to stop in wrong strip

**Radical new approach:** If I cannot distinguish strips using available sensors, the best I can do is:
- Get ALL robots to ONE strip (either one) quickly = score ~0 for that strip's robots
- But which strip? I can't tell without wall color detection

**If C=7 DOES work for walls:** Blue=3, Green=2 should work. My designs have tried this.

**New radical idea:** What if I use the LED beacon strategy more aggressively?
- All robots emit yellow (cle=4)
- Robots on BLACK floor stop and keep emitting
- Robots on GRAY floor move toward yellow-emitting robots (GoToColor yellow=4)
- This creates a self-reinforcing cluster at whichever BLACK strip robots first hit

The problem: they'd cluster at EITHER strip randomly. But with 20 robots, statistical variance means sometimes most end up in the right strip.

**Actually - BEST possible strategy without strip discrimination:**
- Robots on GRAY: move toward any BLACK floor (which means toward either strip wall)
- Once on BLACK: stop and emit a beacon color
- Other robots follow the beacon
- Expected: ~50% reach target → score ~600

**But wait - if C=7 works for walls, GoToColor(blue=3) SHOULD work.** Let me commit hard to this AND the beacon approach.

**New insight on beacon:** If robots stop on BLACK and emit yellow (cle=4), AND moving robots do GoToColor(yellow=4), they'll ALL cluster at whichever strip robots first reached. With 20 robots randomly placed in center, they'll spread to both strips... this won't work well.

**Best bet:** Commit to GoToColor(blue=3) as primary + stop on BLACK, with LED beacon as backup signal. Also try GoAwayColor(green=2).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've run 9 rounds with no score feedback. This is the final round. Let me make my best possible designs.

## Reasoning

**Final analysis of what I know:**
- The mission is to get robots into the BLACK strip bordered by BLUE walls
- Robots start on GRAY (middle)
- BLACK floor exists in BOTH strips - floor color alone is insufficient
- BLUE walls (target) vs GREEN walls (wrong)

**My best hypothesis for what works:**
The beacon approach: robots on BLACK floor stop and emit a colored LED, robots still moving use GoToColor to navigate toward that beacon color. This creates self-organization toward black strips. But it doesn't distinguish target from wrong strip.

**The only reliable discrimination mechanism is C=7 with wall colors.** I'll commit fully to:
- GoToColor(blue=3): move toward blue walls → target strip
- GoAwayColor(green=2): flee green walls → away from wrong strip  
- Stop on BLACK with LED beacon emission (cle=4 yellow)
- Moving robots also use GoToColor(yellow=4) to follow beacons

**My strongest design pattern from rounds 7-9:**
`Explore → [BLACK floor] → Stop+emit-yellow`
combined with
`GoToColor(blue) → [BLACK floor] → Stop+emit-yellow`

**For this final round, I'll include:**
1. Elitism: best designs from previous rounds (beacon + GoToColor blue)
2. Exploitation: small parameter variations
3. Exploration: new topologies

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
